Fiscal policy: Headline surpluses mask a growing investment push 
Kyrgyzstan’s consolidated budget remains comfortably in surplus by 2.5-3.0% of GDP, 
supported by rapid economic growth and strong revenues, but the headline balance 
increasingly understates the expansionary direction of fiscal policy. Direct expenditures 
have been growing faster than GDP-driven tax revenues, largely reflecting the state’s 
infrastructure and investment push, as spending on ‘economic policies’ in the fiscal 
statistics is currently almost 5 percentage points of GDP higher than two years ago, 
eroding the underlying balance of tax revenues and direct expenditures. More 
importantly, once volatile non-tax revenues associated with financial and capital 
transactions are excluded, the fiscal position moves into a widening deficit. This helps 
explain why government borrowing has continued to increase despite the headline 
surplus. 
Nominal public debt has more than doubled from KGS436bn in 2021 to around 
KGS900bn currently, even as rapid nominal GDP growth has brought the debt ratio down 
sharply and more recently kept it broadly stable at around 40% of GDP. Recent 
borrowing, including the recent US$700m Eurobond placement in 2025, reflects 
primarily government financial transactions, including operations involving capital 
injections into state-owned enterprises, as suggested by the recent IMF analysis. The 
growing role of such operations means that the composition and use of new borrowing 
are becoming increasingly important for assessing Kyrgyzstan’s underlying fiscal 
position and sovereign credit story. 
Part of this borrowing reflects an infrastructure-heavy growth model, including the CKU 
railway and Kambarata-1. This raises potential growth if successfully executed, but also 
makes project execution, SOE balance sheets and the structure of external funding 
increasingly important for sovereign investors. 
Fig 42 Kyrgyzstan: Consolidated budget balance   Fig 43 Kyrgyzstan: Key public debt metrics

Source: National sources, IMF, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Inflation and monetary policy: Overheating sends hawkish signals 
Inflation has accelerated well beyond the National Bank's 5-7% target range. Domestic 
tariff increases were an important contributor in the first half of 2026, but price 
pressures have subsequently broadened, with strong demand adding to external food 
and other cost pressures. The relative stability of the som has provided an important 
offset to imported inflation. 
This leaves monetary policy in a difficult position. With inflation broadly matching the 
12% policy rate, monetary conditions do not look particularly tight. Rapid money and 
credit growth and abundant liquidity also point to weak transmission. We expect further 
near-term tightening, followed by scope for partial reversal during 2027 if inflation 
moderates. We also think stronger liquidity management, alongside the policy rate, will 
be important if the National Bank is to bring inflation sustainably back into its target 
range.

Balance of payments and KGS: Strong buffers, vulnerable external accounts 
Som stability masks a much more complicated external position. Kyrgyzstan runs a 
structurally large merchandise trade deficit, currently at US$11bn on a 12-month rolling 
basis, increasingly driven by machinery and other imports from China and other non-CIS 
economies. Some of these imports reflect the domestic investment boom, while part of 
the exceptional trade expansion has been related to regional re-exports. The latter 
appears to have peaked in 2024-2025 and has normalised since then. Meanwhile, trade 
ties with Russia remain deep, with bilateral trade exceeding US$4bn per annum, supporting 
near-term activity but also reinforcing Kyrgyzstan's exposure to Russia-related external risks. 
Remittance inflows provide an important counterweight to the trade deficit. Gross 
inflows remain close to record levels of US$3bn per annum and continue to be 
dominated by Russia (90%), while net remittances of around 11% of GDP provide 
substantial support to household incomes, domestic demand and the FX market. This 
provides a large buffer but leaves Kyrgyzstan heavily exposed to economic and policy 
developments in Russia. 
Fig 46 Kyrgyzstan: Trade balance by region  Fig 47 Kyrgyzstan: Net remittances

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Official BOP data requires particular caution; IMF estimates suggest that unrecorded  
re-exports materially distort the measured current account deficit. Structural current 
account deficits of US$5-7bn per annum require continued external financing, while 
large unrecorded trade and financial flows make the headline current account numbers 
unusually difficult to interpret. FDI and, more recently, portfolio flows have helped 
finance the external funding gap. International reserves have risen sharply in nominal 
terms, but much of the increase reflects gold revaluation; physical gold holdings have 
remained broadly stable. 
For now, these buffers and the managed exchange rate regime should continue 
ensuring som stability, seen since mid-2025. But the combination of a large trade deficit,

Banking sector: Rapid financial deepening brings its own risks 
Loan growth in Kyrgyzstan remains very strong, particularly in retail, which is expanding 
rapidly from a relatively low base, though the pace has recently moderated. Corporate 
loan growth is accelerating and catching up with retail, with FX lending playing a stable 
role. Meanwhile, deposit and broader funding growth has slowed. Most notably, 
corporate deposit inflows slowed materially, and this stands out compared to other 
segments of the banking sector. 
The initial drop appears partly related to large corporate and government payments in the 
middle of the year, but the subsequent weakness may also reflect tighter sanctions 
compliance, including on the A7 payment network and the ruble-linked A757 
cryptocurrency, as well as new restrictions and fees affecting non-resident corporate 
accounts.   
On the lending side, the low starting point means that part of the expansion represents 
welcome financial deepening. However, rapid credit creation is also reinforcing domestic 
demand and weakening monetary policy transmission at a time when inflation is 
already high. Credit quality, bank liquidity and the pace at which lending continues to 
outgrow the funding base are therefore becoming increasingly important macro-
financial indicators to watch. 
Fig 50 Kyrgyzstan: Bank funding indicators  Fig 51 Kyrgyzstan: Bank lending indicators

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Sovereign credit strategy 
While Kyrgyzstan remains an interesting reform story for EM investors, recent 
performance has seen spreads squeeze tight versus peers. Headline fiscal metrics look 
strong but mask increased spending and borrowing needs, which could drive further

Economic overview 
Uzbekistan remains our preferred medium-term CCA growth and reform story, but 
strong recent performance also brings increasing signs of overheating and leaves 
important macro imbalances unresolved. Growth remains well above potential, 
inflation is still above target and persistent twin deficits coexist with continued high 
state involvement in the economy and a much-reduced liquid component of the 
sovereign wealth fund. At the same time, reform and privatisation momentum, high 
real rates and strong capital inflows have supported the soum, while unusually 
restrained physical gold exports leave potential for additional FX supply if sales 
normalise. 
We therefore retain a stronger-than-consensus medium-term view on UZS and see 
scope for gradual monetary easing, but a durable break from its historical 
depreciation trend requires further disinflation, fiscal discipline and evidence that 
private capital inflows can remain strong as the current reform and privatisation 
push matures. 
Economic activity: Accelerating beyond potential 
Uzbekistan’s GDP growth has been exceptionally strong recently, supported by domestic 
demand catching up from a low base. While activity is expected to moderate from the 
current above-potential levels, Uzbekistan should remain one of the fastest-growing 
economies in emerging markets. Strong investment spending, industrial activity and 
ongoing reforms continue to support expansion, while positive demographic trends 
provide an additional tailwind.  
A gradual slowdown in the GDP growth is likely to reflect normalisation in the activity 
trends, with growth remaining comfortably above regional peers. The immediate 
challenge is managing overheating as consumption and investment continue to grow 
rapidly.  
Fig 53 Uzbekistan: GDP growth composition  Fig 54 Uzbekistan: Key activity indicators

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Fiscal policy: Pace of consolidation moderated 
Fiscal consolidation is continuing, with the general government deficit narrowing to 1.1% 
of GDP in the four quarters to 3Q26, from 4.1% in 2023. The pace of improvement has 
slowed recently, partly because unusually low gold exports have constrained revenues, 
which we expect to be temporary. At the same time, expenditure growth is catching up 
with revenue growth, pointing to a more structural challenge for Uzbekistan’s fiscal policy.

The government also has strong incentives to maintain fiscal restraint. Domestic 
demand is already running hot, while several years of budget deficits have reduced the 
UFRD's liquid component from 17% of GDP in 2019 to around 3% in 2025. We see fiscal 
deficits limited to around 1% of GDP on the three-year horizon, with a growing share of 
debt financing and privatisation as a source of financing, which should be taken 
positively by portfolio investors. 
The privatisation programme, though stemming largely from the fiscal needs, is gaining 
importance as a capital-markets story. Following the UzNIF transaction, the widening 
pipeline of state assets creates scope for international capital raising. 
Fig 55 Uzbekistan: Consolidated budget balance   Fig 56 Uzbekistan: Sovereign fund composition

Inflation and monetary policy: Scope for gradual easing 
Over the past couple of years, inflation in Uzbekistan has moderated substantially from 
previous highs, aided by the recent fiscal consolidation, and this has created room for a 
cautious monetary policy easing cycle. However, current CPI remains above the central 
bank's long-term objective, while strong domestic demand, administrative tariff 
adjustments and global food and commodity risks argue against aggressive rate cuts.  
We therefore expect the central bank to deliver only gradual easing from a still-
restrictive level. Real rates remain relatively attractive and should continue to support 
macro stability while allowing policymakers to balance growth objectives against the 
need for further disinflation.

Our stronger-than-consensus medium-term view on the soum continues to be 
reinforced by recent developments. High real rates, gold prices, an expected catch-up in 
physical gold exports and growing foreign participation in Uzbekistan's privatisation 
programme provide important support for the balance of payments and FX market. The 
investment pipeline continues to broaden geographically, with a recently announced

package of US-linked projects potentially worth US$12bn upon implementation, spanning 
transport, critical minerals, infrastructure and financial services. This should underpin the 
resilience of the UZS despite the country's persistent trade and current-account deficits.  
Uzbekistan’s physical gold exports have been significantly restrained this year, at just 18 
tonnes in January-September 2026 compared with 85 tonnes in the same period of 
2025. This means that the benefit from the gold price rally has so far been limited on the 
external trade side. The slight appreciation of the soum against major currencies this 
year appears to have been driven largely by capital inflows, partly supported by high 
real rates, as has been the case in Kazakhstan. 
Fig 59 Uzbekistan: CBRU monthly gold exports  Fig 60 Uzbekistan: Balance of payments

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

The reason for the unusually low sales has not been formally explained. However, the 
simultaneous increase in the central bank's physical gold holdings – to 453.5 tonnes in 
October - indicates deliberate retention amid continued extraction. The significant 
restraint on gold exports so far this year suggests some upside potential for the soum if 
export volumes recover towards their historical range of 80-120 tonnes per year. This 
catch-up in delayed gold exports is one of the factors we see supporting the currency 
over the medium term. 
However, the longer-term outlook is less certain, as Uzbekistan continues to run sizeable 
twin deficits, while inflation remains above target. As a result, we view the UZS positively 
in the medium-term but believe a durable break from the historical depreciation trend 
would require sustained progress in reducing macro imbalances and deepening 
structural reforms. 
Sovereign credit strategy 
The fundamental picture for Uzbekistan has improved significantly in recent years, with 
narrowing twin deficits amid solid growth reflected in rating upgrades. Fitch’s positive 
outlook suggests there is still scope for improvement to continue, while the stronger 
credit profile should increase the country’s resilience to the current energy shock. An 
expected catch-up in gold exports, alongside ongoing capital inflows linked to the 
privatisation pipeline should keep the momentum strong, and we see the potential for 
more fundamental upside.

## Document 327: China’s October domestic activity data disappoints across the board

Source PDF: all report pdf/China’s October domestic activity data disappoints across the board.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-11-16
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

China’s retail sales, ﬁxed-asset investment, and industrial
production all came in weaker than expected in October, a
worrying start to the fourth quarter. Signs of further
stabilisation in property prices oﬀer some rays of hope, but the
domestic economy would beneﬁt from more policy support
Lynn Song
Beijing, China
Retail sales slowed by more than expected
in October
China's retail sales slowed to 0.4% year-on-year in October, down
from 1.1% in September, softer than expectations (market: 0.8%,

ING: 0.7%).
Three categories dragged retail sales with double-digit YoY declines
in October. The ﬁrst was gold and jewellery sales (-28.7%), which
plummeted amid falling gold prices. The second was auto sales
(-21.4%), as demand cooled after years of policy support pulled
forward purchases and accelerated China’s shift to electric vehicles.
Your privacy is important

our Cookie policy.

Targeting Cookies
Save preferences Accept all

### Page 2

Finally, building and decoration materials (-11.2%) were under
pressure as continued property market sluggishness limited
renovation demand.
Other categories fared better, but optimism remains limited. Even
categories that had performed strongly earlier in the year, such as
communication appliances (6.5%) and tobacco & alcohol (2.5%),
saw growth slow in October.
Consumption momentum remains tepid heading into the last leg
of 2026. While a more supportive base eﬀect could help the data in

the months ahead, this year will likely mark the worst for
consumption since the pandemic. Moving forward, we will likely
need new methods to support consumption, as the trade-in policy
has turned from tailwind to headwind. Persistently weak consumer
conﬁdence remains the biggest obstacle to a sustained recovery in
consumption, making measures that restore household conﬁdence
essential to any eﬀective policy response. Two main channels to
restore conﬁdence are stabilising asset prices and encouraging

stronger wage growth.
Retail sales growth pulled back in October
Industrial production slows despite solid
external demand
Industrial production slowed to 4.8% YoY in October, down from
5.5% in September, missing expectations (market: 5.3%, ING: 5.5%).
Year-to-date growth remained at 5.3%, keeping industrial
Your privacy is important
At ING we respect your right to choose how your data is used.
You can review and change your preferences at any time in

our Cookie policy.
Essential
Performance Cookies
Targeting Cookies

### Page 3

production a relatively bright spot, although October’s data
suggest momentum is softening.
Manufacturing outperformed the headline, growing 5.4% YoY,
compared with 0.7% for mining and 4.4% for utilities. High-tech
manufacturing remained the key source of strength, accelerating to
17.6% YoY. At the industry level, computer, communication and
other electronic equipment manufacturing rose 19.4%, while
special equipment increased 11.5% followed by rail, ships &

aerospace at 9.2%, general equipment at 8.5%, and electrical
machinery at 8.1%.
Solid external demand continued to support industrial activity.
Export delivery value rose 18.6% YoY in nominal terms, remaining
consistent with resilient merchandise exports. Product-level data
showed similar strength in selective high-tech segments.
Semiconductor production rose 21.9% YoY, industrial robots
increased 26.0%, and NEV production grew 17.1%. Yet strength

remained uneven, with microcomputer and smartphone output
recording double-digit declines.
Weakness was also evident in the traditional economy and
domestic demand-linked sectors. Total vehicle production fell
-7.7%, led by a -23.7% decline in passenger cars. Property- and
construction-linked products remained under pressure, with ﬂat
glass down -16.0%, steel products falling -5.1% and cement
declining -1.3%.
Overall, industrial production remained supported by high-tech

manufacturing and exports-related products. However, October’s
slowdown and persistent weakness in domestic-demand sectors
point to the continued unevenness of industrial growth.
External demand continues to support
industrial production

### Page 4

Fixed asset investment growth
unexpectedly deteriorated
FAI growth fell sharply to a new low of -8.1% YoY ytd in October,
down from -7.2% YoY ytd. This came in signiﬁcantly worse than
expected (market: -7.0%, ING: -6.7%) and shows investment
remains under pressure despite recent eﬀorts to accelerate ﬁscal
spending and the use of bond proceeds.
Continued weakness on the private side was expected, but the data
came in even weaker than anticipated, with private-led investment

falling to -11.4% YoY ytd, down from -10.4% YoY ytd in the ﬁrst
three quarters of the year. The bigger factor was the public side.
After seeing an uptick in public-led investment in last month's data,
public-led investment fell to -4.0% YoY ytd, down from -3.2% YoY
ytd over the ﬁrst three quarters of the year. This month's data
suggests the September rise was a blip rather than the start of a
recovery.
In terms of the subcategories, there weren't many silver linings. Hi-

tech FAI remained the outperformer (4.0%), but it decelerated for a
second consecutive month. The contraction of real estate (-21.6%),
infrastructure (-5.1%), and manufacturing (-2.9%) FAI worsened
again this month.
Weak investment looks to remain a signiﬁcant overhang on the
economy.

### Page 5

FAI slowed across the board in October
Property market shows further signs of
stabilisation
China's 70-city property price index continued to fall in aggregate,
with new home prices down -0.15% month-on-month and used
home prices down -0.24%, both broadly unchanged from last
month. Property prices have seen 41 consecutive months of
sequential declines, but the monthly drops have slowed this year.
The city-level breakdown continues to oﬀer cause for cautious

optimism amid the data. In October, 21 of 70 cities saw new home
prices stabilise or pick up, while 12 of 70 cities saw used home
prices stabilise or rise, the highest proportion in 5 months. Tier 1
cities continued to show a price recovery, while this has spread to
several tier 2 cities as well. Take Shanghai, for example: secondary
market prices have now picked up for 9 straight months, the
longest streak since the property bust began. After a few fakeouts,

this time the recovery at China's core looks more convincing.
In our 10 questions for China in 2027 report, we discuss whether
the property market's drag is expected to fade. The stabilisation of
prices in tier-one cities is an important ﬁrst step toward restoring
conﬁdence. However, a broader recovery in the property sector will
require not only ﬁrmer prices but also a more convincing reduction
in housing inventories before investment can rebound.

The scenario, forecasts and nowcasts of the Economic Research 
– 
16 
Novem
ber 2026

### Page 2

UNITED STATES 
The US economy is expected to grow above its potential in 2026, with an average annual growth rate of 2.2%, close to the 2025 rate (2.1%). 
This apparent resilience to the uncertainty, tariff and energy shocks, together with above-trend productivity growth, masks K-shaped growth, 
driven by investment linked to AI-optimism and consumption by the wealthiest amid a wealth effect (historically high stock market valuations). 
Inflation overshooting is set to continue (3.5% in 2026) at least through 2028, largely because of the rise in oil prices and tariffs – although the 
impact of these latter appears to be less significant than expected. The labour market is showing clear signs of improvement that should bring 
the unemployment rate down toward 4.0%. Given this shift in price and employment risks amid dynamic growth, the FOMC proceeded with a 
first rate hike at September’s meeting and we expect two further 25bp increases in December 2026 and January 2027, bringing the Fed Funds 
target range at 4.25% - 4.50%.

CHINA 
Economic growth rebounded to 4.5% y/y in Q3 2026 (1.2% q/q) after 4.3% y/y in Q2 and 5.0% in Q1. It is projected to reach 4.6% in 2026 as a 
whole, down from 5% in 2025. Growth remains characterised by a K-shaped trajectory, and the gap between the strong performance of exports 
and the fragility of sectors that rely on domestic demand continues to widen. Retail sales barely increased in the first ten months of 2026, the 
crisis in the property sector continues and household confidence remains low. Yet new fiscal policy measures could help support investment 
and private consumption in the very short term. Deflationary pressures are persisting, given weakness in domestic demand. Energy prices and 
electronic goods prices are increasing, producer price inflation is accelerating (5.7% y/y in October), but consumer price inflation remains low 
(0.7% y/y).

EUROZONE 
The economy has shown resilience in the face of the energy shock: GDP grew by 0.4% q/q in Q3, and the 1.0% growth carry-over for 2026 
supports our forecast of 1.1% for the year as a whole (compared to 1.3% in 2025) with the risk tilted to the upside. Growth is expected to 
strengthen to 1.6% in 2027, bolstered by increased spending on defence and infrastructure, as well as the global investment cycle in AI. Annual 
average inflation is thus expected to reach 3.1% in 2026 (from 2.1% in 2025), and 3.2% in 2027. Given persistent inflation and resilient growth, 
we anticipate another 25bp hike from the ECB in December 2026, bringing the deposit rate to 2.75%, followed by an extended pause. That 
said, stronger than expected second-round effects could lead to more tightening than our current baseline.

ECONOMIC SCENARIO AND FORECAST

FRANCE 
GDP contracted by 0.1% q/q in Q3 2026 (following stagnation in Q2), weighed down by a decline in aeronautics production (delay in the 
delivery of inputs) but supported by service sector output and purchases of electric vehicles. Overall, growth is expected to slow to 0.3% in 
2026 (after 0.9% in 2025) before rebounding to 0.8% in 2027. In parallel, inflation should reach 2.5% in 2026 (compared with 0.9% in 2025), 
before edging down slightly in 2027 (2.4%).

UNITED KINGDOM 
UK GDP growth would accelerate to 1.6% in 2026 (1.2% in 2025). The economy has so far weathered both the energy price shock and domestic 
political volatility, boosted by domestic demand, notably investment. After a series of strong prints from Q1 to Q3, real GDP growth is expected 
to decelerate in the last quarter of the year, as higher energy prices should feed inflation. It should then slow to 1.3% in 2027 on the back of 
higher inflation and more restrictive monetary conditions. Inflation should climb to 3.9% in Q4 2026, peaking in Q1 2027 (at 4.5%) before 
gradually slowing. Still, it is set to remain stubbornly above target, leading to mounting second-round effects risks. In response, the BoE has 
finally raised its Bank Rate to 4.00% early November. If a second-rate hike looks likely in February, this is not, for now, our call and our baseline 
scenario projects that diminishing inflation will allow monetary easing to resume in the second half of 2027, with two cuts taking the Bank 
Rate down to 3.50% by year-end.

JAPAN 
Following 1.2% in 2025, Japan's GDP growth is expected to decelerate to 0.9% in 2026, before rebounding to 1.0% in 2027. Higher inflation 
and production costs associated with the energy shock are expected to weigh on the economy’s overall performance. On the other hand, the 
latter is supported by fiscal policy and AI-related investment. Inflation has generally overshot the 2% y/y target since 2022. Accordingly, the 
Bank of Japan initiated a cautious process of “adjustment in the degree of monetary accommodation” in 2024, lifting the policy rate to 1.25% 
so far (previously negative) – the highest since 1995. We expect two more hikes by the end of Q1 2027, followed by a return to a more cautious 
pace until the terminal rate of +2.5% is reached in H2 2028. Japan is facing long-term rates pressure, illustrated by historically high 10- and  
30-year yields, probably fueled by the level of public debt and the pace of monetary adjustment.

EXCHANGE RATES 
We expect Fed hikes to temporarily offset structural headwinds and support the dollar. EUR/USD would target 1.14 by end-2026, before the 
dollar's structural downtrend resumes toward 1.20 by end-2027, although political and fiscal risk may weigh on the euro next year. The USD/JPY 
appreciation would continue but at a more moderate pace, with the BoJ still behind the curve despite its hikes. By contrast, GBP/USD would 
see relative support underpinned by a modest fiscal risk premium under the new Labour government, and resilient growth.

## Document 470: Who finances private credit in the United States_ What the Federal Reserve’s data reveal (2026-10-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Who finances private credit in the United States_ What the Federal Reserve’s data reveal (2026-10-28).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-28
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

28 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
WHO FINANCES PRIVATE CREDIT IN THE UNITED STATES? WHAT THE FEDERAL RESERVE’S DATA REVEAL
Céline Choulet
SOURCE: FEDERAL RESERVE (ENHANCED FINANCIAL ACCOUNTS), BNP PARIBAS
The private credit market raises many questions, and its lack of transparency is not helping. Official public data remain scarce. The available
estimates come mainly from private sources, whose methodologies vary widely. The Financial Accounts of the Federal Reserve (Fed) now shed

new light on the matter. They reveal that private credit finances 7.1% of US corporate debt through households (31% of funds’ total financing),
insurers and pension funds (20%), and banks (19%).
Private credit can take several forms. The most common is loans granted by closed-end funds 1 to small and medium-sized, unlisted and highly
indebted companies. These loans are generally variable-rate, with flexible terms based on cash flow rather than a measure of profitability. While

this asset class has inherent vulnerabilities 2 (Bank of France, 2025), it is also particularly well-suited to financing innovative and long-term
projects (such as digital 3, infrastructure, renewable energy and healthcare), especially when combined with an equity stake in the company. Its
slower growth in Europe likely explains, in part, the region’s lag in innovation financing.
Private credit finances 7.1% of US corporate debt…
Private credit has grown rapidly in the United States over the past decade and has established itself as an alternative to bank loans for

a category of borrowers. It now accounts for 17.3% of the total outstanding loans to American non-financial corporations (NFCs) 4 and 7.1%
1 Strictly speaking, a closed-end fund has a fixed term during which investors cannot withdraw their invested capital. However, the sector has diversified since 2020. The Fed distinguishes
between 1) strictly closed-end private funds (private debt funds), 2) business development companies, which may have an indefinite lifespan and allow for occasional redemptions of

shares, and 3) interval and tender offer funds, which offer the possibility of scheduled redemptions.
2 Notably, high credit risk, high leverage, difficulty in valuing loans, opaque credit ratings, occasionally complex credit structures, high concentration within the sector and increasing
liquidity risk.
3 In 2025, nearly half of the direct loans made by private credit funds went to the tech sector (software, data centres and artificial intelligence) (Abbassi et al., 2026).

4 No comparable data exist for the Eurozone. According to France Invest (2026) data, private credit issued by French funds accounted for 4% of annual bank credit flows to NFCs in 2025.
Who finances US private credit funds?

2

a changing
world

of their debt. Approximately one-third (35%) is managed by strictly closed-end funds, with another third (36%) by semi-open-end funds
that allow occasional early redemptions. The remaining third is held on the balance sheets of non-resident private debt funds (25%) and
securitisation vehicles (4%).
… through banks, insurers and pension funds, and households

In yet another example of the lengthening of the credit intermediation chain in the United States (following that of the mortgage market),
banks are among the main creditors of private credit funds (65% of their debt and 19% of their total financing). The low risk weighting
of loans granted by banks to these vehicles, compared to that of direct bank loans to non-financial corporations, is undoubtedly a factor
(Chernenko et al., 2024). The holdings of insurers and pension funds – consisting mainly of shares in strictly closed-end funds – make

them among the leading investors in private debt funds (30% of issued shares and 20% of total financing). This asset class offers them the
opportunity to diversify their portfolios and attractive returns. The rise of semi-open-end funds has expanded the investor base to include
retail investors (31% of total financing), albeit at the cost of increased liquidity risk, as evidenced by the significant wave of redemptions in
late 2025 and early 2026 5. American households directly hold 70% of the equity issued by semi-open funds and 22% of the shares issued

by strictly closed funds 6.

## Document 472: Why hasn’t there been a major financial crisis in recent years_ (2026-11-03)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Why hasn’t there been a major financial crisis in recent years_ (2026-11-03).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-11-03
Pages: 19
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

Why hasn’t there been a major 
financial crisis in recent years?

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Macro Strategist

Peter Sidorov, CFA 
Senior Economist

Camilla Siazon 
Research Analyst

November 3, 2026 
— The economist Hyman Minsky warned, in essence, that “stability is destabilising”. He 
argued that a lengthy period of financial stability could be problematic, as it induced 
risk-taking and complacency amongst investors, laying the foundation for the next 
crisis. For example, lenders relax their terms and borrowers are more willing to extend 
themselves, as nobody wants to miss out. This dynamic makes economies inherently 
prone to financial panics and boom-bust cycles. 
— But since the 2008 crisis, and the subsequent sovereign crisis in the Eurozone, we 
have yet to see another financial crisis happen on a scale that had a lasting market 
and macroeconomic impact. There have been clear moments of concern, as in early 
2023 with the regional bank turmoil, but nothing that has caused a lasting impact. So 
this led us to ask why. 
— In this paper, we examine why the post-GFC era has seen fewer financial crises. 
Explanations include the reforms made after 2008 to avoid a repeat, the lower 
proportion of financial assets held by the banking system, as well as the speedier 
reaction of policymakers when wobbles have occurred. We then consider a few 
candidates that could cause the next crisis, including AI vulnerabilities, private credit, 
and stretched fiscal positions. 
— Ultimately, financial crises haven’t been abolished, but the reforms of recent years 
have made it harder for shocks to propagate through the traditional banking system. 
Moreover, policymakers have become far more inclined to intervene when initial signs 
of stress have emerged.

### Page 2

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
Section 1: What is a financial crisis, and what causes them?
There isn’t a single technical definition of a “financial crisis”, and they come in many
different types. Commonly, we think of banking crises like 2008, but they can also
encompass currency/balance of payments crises, debt crises and other forms too.
Given currency and sovereign crises have been more common in emerging markets
(the UK's ERM crisis in 1992 and the euro crisis being the two most notable DM

occurrences), historical analysis in developed economies has tended to focus
heavily on banking crises. Non-bank funding or market-liquidity crises are often not
categorised separately in the literature, but they have become more common in
recent years. This includes the "dash-for-cash" stress at the onset of the Covid
pandemic in March 2020 and the 2022 LDI crisis in the UK.
But for all the different types of financial crisis, there are common themes between

them. Often, they are accompanied by asset bubbles that burst in a non-linear
fashion. We generally see a severe tightening in credit conditions, which in turn
flows through into a broader economic contraction. Frequently, there are
contagion-like dynamics that can be difficult to stop. And in the modern world, they
usually require policymakers to step in, stabilising the situation with interventions.
At risk of oversimplification, we note four factors that determine the emergence and

the severity of a financial crisis. The first three are well-established in the financial
literature, sometimes pooled together into a build-up and a crisis phase
(Brunnermeier & Oehmke 2013), while the fourth has become increasingly
important over the past 15-20 years:
1.  Underlying imbalances. Bubbles and financial imbalances build during
the run-up phase, leaving accumulated risks that materialise when the
crisis erupts. This is the build-up typically described by the early phases of

Kindleberger's model of financial crises.
2.  Trigger. This is the shock that turns an existing vulnerability into clear
financial stress. These are often very difficult to predict and we mostly leave
these outside the focus of this report.
3.  Amplification and financial system fragility. Various factors determine
whether a shock propagates through the financial system, and the
intensity of that shock. These include leverage, liquidity and maturity

mismatches, interconnectedness, and the concentration of exposures.
Other channels in the financial system can then amplify the shock,
including bank runs, fire sales and margin calls, and negative net worth
effects, sovereign–bank loops etc.
4.  Intervention/policy response. This has been increasingly important in
recent decades as the size and scope of policy response to crises has
increased. The outright size can vary, and intervention can occur across

several dimensions, ranging from direct intervention in financial markets,
to counter-cyclical macroeconomic policy that mitigates the impact on the
real economy.
Clearly, these factors are not independent and the interaction between them is what
determines the severity of a financial crisis.

### Page 3

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 3
One important aspect is the joint role that underlying imbalances and amplification
play. While theoretical models such as Diamond & Dybvig highlight the potential for
a bank run that triggers a broad crisis, empirical research shows that in practice, it
is exceedingly rare for a bank run to lead to bank failure in the absence of an
underlying solvency issue (Correia, Luck and Verner 2026). In other words, the

presence of amplification channels alone does not lead to a crisis.
Conversely, in the absence of amplification, it is possible to get significant
turbulence in asset prices, but without a financial crisis. For instance, a bear market,
wider credit spreads, or even a recession do not in and of themselves imply there
is a financial crisis. This was seen clearly with the bursting of the dot com bubble,
a severe equity decline but not a financial crisis, though it did cause a recession. At

the same time, in the chart below, we can see several US equity bear markets that
took place outside of recessions. On other occasions, however, a “Minsky
Moment” can then happen, where even a seemingly minor event can trigger a high-
profile incident that cascades across the financial system.
Figure 1: S&P 500 drawdowns over time, with shaded recessions showing select episodes when the index fell over 20%
without coinciding with a recession
Source :Bloomberg Finance LP, NBER, Deutsche Bank Research

Looking back through history, we can see how variation in these factors has led to
different regimes over time in terms of frequency/severity of financial factors.

### Page 4

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
Section 2. Why have financial crises been more or less likely
in certain periods of history?
1. Pre-WWII: More frequent financial instability, albeit under a very
different system.
Financial instability was a recurrent feature of the pre-WWII era. During the 19th
and early 20th centuries, there were frequent panics that often led to major slumps.
For instance, the Panic of 1873 sparked a period often known as the “Long

Depression”, which extended into the 1890s for many countries.
In part, financial panics occurred more frequently because policy operated under
very different settings. For instance, the Gold Standard as the monetary system
constrained a countercyclical policy response and transmitted deflation
internationally, while falling prices increased real debt burdens. Moreover, the
Federal Reserve wasn’t established until 1913, and the lack of an effective lender of

last resort meant there was a private intervention during the US Panic of 1907. The
first wave of globalisation also brought new vulnerabilities. Between 1870 and
1913, many emerging economies accumulated debts denominated in gold or
foreign currency, leaving them exposed when capital inflows reversed or exchange
rates weakened.
The biggest and most famous crisis occurred in 1929, when the Wall Street Crash
of that year sparked the Great Depression. The length and depth of that crisis helped

to stimulate the study of modern macroeconomics, with huge efforts to prevent a
repeat over the years ahead. Indeed, the failures of the response to the Great
Depression played a large role in the much swifter response to the 2008 financial
crisis, helping to avoid a second Great Depression.
That experience established much of the modern financial safety net that exists to
this day. For instance in the US, the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation was

established in 1933. The US and many other countries came off the Gold Standard
as well in this period. There were also regulatory reforms, and the Glass-Steagall Act
separated out commercial from investment banking.
2. 1944-1971: The Bretton Woods era coincided with fewer financial
crises
Around the end of WWII, economic policymakers sought to create an international
monetary system for the post-war era. The relative financial calm that followed

reflected a combination of restrictions on international capital movement, tightly
regulated domestic financial systems, and a system of fixed exchange rates that
aimed to provide stability and prevent the competitive devaluations that occurred
in the 1930s. This saw exchange rates pegged against the US Dollar, which in turn
was convertible to gold at $35 per ounce.
At the same time, capital controls helped preserve monetary policy flexibility, while

maintaining the system of fixed exchange rates. These controls had several
downsides but they also insulated countries from “sudden stop” crises that can
occur, when capital rapidly flees a country. Moreover, capital controls prevented
the rapid buildup of cross-border systemic leverage. So it was more difficult to
generate a global financial crisis in the same way.

### Page 5

In the Bretton Woods

Source : Finaeon, Deutsche Bank
Alongside capital controls, there were also domestic reforms in the aftermath of the
Great Depression, which sought to avoid a repeat. As mentioned earlier, the US
Glass-Steagall Act of 1933 separated commercial banking from investment

banking. Another example of the era’s regulated financial system in the US was
Regulation Q, which imposed maximum interest rates that commercial banks
could pay on deposits. That meant depositors could not search for yield and cause
a run on deposits as easily. Another rationale was that with institutions not
competing for deposits, it would avoid a compression in the margin between the
rates banks paid on their lending and borrowing, which would disincentivise a push

into riskier investments.
Finally, economic growth was also stronger in this period, with the period between
WWII and the 1973 oil shock often described as the “Golden Age”. This played a role
in avoiding financial crises too, as faster growth helped keep debt default rates
down, avoiding the kind of “trigger” events above that might spark a financial crisis.
Coupled with financial repression, strong growth also helped to bring down the
high post-war public debt burdens.

Over time however, the peg between the dollar and gold found itself under growing
strain. The economist Robert Triffin identified the “Triffin dilemma”. Supplying the
world with dollar reserves resulted in outstanding dollar liabilities around the world
that exceeded the gold reserves in the US, weakening confidence that dollars could
ultimately be redeemed for gold. Conversely, restricting dollar supply to protect
those reserves and preserve convertibility risked depriving the system of the

liquidity needed to support trade and growth.
These tensions intensified in the late 1960s amid expansionary US policies,
including spending on the Vietnam War and Great Society programmes. The
continued drain in US gold reserves eventually led the US to suspend the
convertibility of the US Dollar into gold, effectively ending the Bretton Woods
system. Initially, there were attempts to bring some kind of fixed-exchange rate
system back, but the world of fiat currencies (with no link to a precious metal)

persists to this day.

### Page 6

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
3. 1971-2008: Financial crises become more frequent after the
breakdown of Bretton Woods to the Global Financial Crisis
After the breakdown of the Bretton Woods system, financial crises became a more
regular occurrence. While the end of the gold convertibility did not by itself trigger
future crises, it opened the door for wider financial liberalisation, with the resulting
rise in capital mobility and looser domestic financial regulation then helping to

facilitate a global build-up of imbalances.
The 1980s and 1990s were a period of frequent crisis events. The transition away
from capital controls meant that capital could flow more freely across borders.
However, this could also cause a “sudden stop” if investors suddenly pulled their
capital out of a country, contributing to bank runs and currency collapses.
Moreover, many emerging markets borrowed in foreign currencies like the US
Dollar, meaning that a depreciation in their own currency would see this USD-

denominated debt surge in local currency terms. This meant it was a particular
problem if US interest rates were increasing, as it became harder for many
emerging markets to repay their debts. This was a contributing factor behind the
Latin American debt crisis in the early 1980s. And the rate hikes also had domestic
ramifications, being a contributing factor behind the Savings and Loan crisis in the
United States.
With capital flowing more freely, that also played a role in the 1994 Mexican Peso

Crisis, and the 1997 Asian Financial Crisis. A sudden reversal in capital flows led to
crisis and ultimately recession in many countries. The following year, the Russian
financial crisis then took place, and fears grew in the United States about the wider
ramifications, as the hedge fund Long-Term Capital Management (LTCM) came
under pressure. Indeed, this period saw 75bps of pre-emptive rate cuts from the
Federal Reserve.
This volatility also saw the Fed proactively seek to step in and smooth out the

economic volatility. This was broadly successful, but in the process also helped
engender complacency and a further buildup of leverage. Indeed, after the dot com
bubble burst, the federal funds rate was cut to just 1% in mid-2003, where it
remained until mid-2004.
This period saw clear optimism that another Great Depression was highly unlikely.
For instance, Nobel Prize-winning economist Robert Lucas argued in his 2003 AEA
Presidential Address that “macroeconomics in this original sense has succeeded:

Its central problem of depression prevention has been solved, for all practical
purposes, and has in fact been solved for many decades.”
However, as we now know, the 2008 financial crisis occurred, driving the biggest
global downturn since the Great Depression of the 1930s. It turned out that Minsky
had been correct, and a lengthy period without a crisis had once again helped to
induce complacency, laying the foundations for a repeat.

Section 3: The Post-2008 world: Why have we not had
another big financial crisis?
Since the Global Financial Crisis of 2008, we have thankfully avoided a major
financial crisis. Despite clear moments of stress, most notably around the collapse
of SVB in March 2023, we have not seen a similar wave of bank collapses on that
scale. There are several components to this.
First, post-GFC deleveraging cleared out risks in the system, with countries and
sectors that had excess indebtedness before the GFC experiencing pronounced

declines in leverage.
Second, there have been reforms explicitly designed to avoid a repeat of 2008.
After the crisis happened, there was a clear recognition that gaps in regulation had
exacerbated the crisis, leading to effects that cascaded across the financial system.
For instance, the Basel III reforms required banks to hold more equity, with a
minimum requirement for Common Equity Tier 1 (CET1) capital. They also
introduced the liquidity coverage ratio, which requires banks to hold enough high-

quality liquid assets to ensure they can cover their net cash outflows under a 30-day
stressed scenario. In addition, the net stable funding ratio was brought in, requiring
banks to have sufficient stable funding that exceeded the required amount of stable
funding for a one-year stressed scenario.
At the national level, further reforms were also introduced. In the US, the Dodd-
Frank Act was introduced, with measures including the Volcker Rule that restricted

banks from engaging in proprietary trading, and required the Federal Reserve to
perform an annual stress test of large institutions. Meanwhile in the European
Union, which also experienced a sovereign debt crisis, reforms included a “Single
Supervisory Mechanism”, whereby banking supervision was brought under the
ECB, ensuring harmonised regulatory standards.

### Page 8

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
Third, the share of financial assets held by banks has fallen since the 2008 crisis.
This hasn’t eliminated risk, but commercial banks are funded by retail deposits that
can quickly fly. By contrast, non-bank financial institutions (NBFIs), e.g. hedge
funds, are not as prone to these bank run dynamics. For instance, a private credit
closed-ended fund is not one where investors can simply redeem their funds on

demand.
We can see this shift in the data. Looking at the FSB’s group of 21 plus the Eurozone,
we can see that the share of assets held by banks has fallen from a peak of 46% in
2008, to 38% in 2024. By contrast, the share held by NBFIs has risen from 43% to
51%.
Figure 5: Share of Assets of Financial Corporations (FSB 21 + EA Group)
35%

Source :Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank

Fourth, the lessons of 2008 have meant that when the financial system has had
wobbles since, policymakers have acted with incredible speed to stop the
turmoil.
This was most obvious when the Covid-19 pandemic began. Whilst a pandemic
was very different to a financial crisis, there was initially huge turmoil in financial
markets. Indeed, the S&P 500 fell by more than a third in just over a month, a decline
at a speed and scale unseen since the Great Depression. Meanwhile, US HY credit

spreads soared to levels last seen around the financial crisis, and at the height of the
turmoil, there was a huge spike in real yields, despite the economy entering a
recession.
Against this backdrop, central banks wasted no time in acting. For instance, the Fed
delivered an emergency 50bp rate cut, followed by another 100bp rate cut.
Moreover, they swiftly resumed Quantitative Easing, and announced the
establishment of multiple new facilities to ensure the smooth functioning of credit

markets. These included a Primary Market Corporate Credit Facility, to support new
bond and loan issuance, along with a Secondary Market Corporate Credit facility,
which offered liquidity for outstanding corporate bonds. Meanwhile, a third facility,
called the Term Asset-Backed Securities Loan Facility, was set up to enable the
issuance of asset-backed securities. Similarly in Europe, the ECB set up a new
Pandemic Emergency Purchase Programme (PEPP), which ultimately had a total

envelope of €1.85tn.
Whilst the pandemic caused a huge economic shock, the swift action meant it did
not translate into a financial one, and the brief tightening in credit conditions was

### Page 9

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
unwound over the coming months.
A similar pattern was seen during the regional banking turmoil of March 2023. This
was a financial panic, which initially began with the collapse of Silicon Valley Bank.
However, this began to spread, and Signature Bank also failed. Then in Europe,
Credit Suisse came under investor scrutiny and saw large deposit outflows, which
culminated in a purchase by UBS that included guarantees from the Swiss

government. This coincided with huge market moves, with the 2yr Treasury yield
posting its biggest daily decline in over 40 years, with a 61bps decline on March 13.
However, policymakers swiftly moved to stop any contagion. The US Treasury,
Federal Reserve and the FDIC released a joint statement stating that all deposits at
SVB would be protected, and that the Federal Reserve would provide additional
funding to ensure banks could meet the needs of depositors. Meanwhile, the Fed’s

balance sheet briefly expanded again, despite the fact it had begun the process of
quantitative tightening. These actions ultimately meant the crisis proved contained
and did not lead to a wave of further bank collapses in the manner of 2008.

Fifth, policymakers have also responded to recent shocks with huge fiscal

transfers, meaning that debt has migrated from private towards sovereign
balance sheets.
Whilst global debt has risen slightly over the last decade, if you dig deeper into the
numbers, that’s entirely driven by a rise in government debt. If we compare the
statistics for Q2 2026, relative to Q2 2016, there’s actually been a decline in debt for
households, financial corporations, and nonfinancial corporations. The only one of
the four segments that’s gone up in the last decade is government debt.

In relative terms, the transformation is even bigger if we look back to 2007, before
the Global Financial Crisis kicked off. Back then, government debt was the smallest
of the four categories, but because of the GFC and the pandemic, it’s now become
the biggest of the four categories.
That in turn has led to separate issues, with government bond yields at multi-year
highs, alongside rising net interest spending. So the fiscal space has become
limited. However, public balance sheets don’t face the same kind of system-wide

run that a bank would.

Overall, the post-2008 experience offers three key forward-looking lessons. First,
stronger bank balance sheets and the shift towards non-bank finance suggest

future stress may originate outside banks. As such, historical analyses centred on
banking crises may provide a less complete guide. Second, policymakers have
become increasingly effective at containing liquidity pressures and amplification.
And third, the shift in debt towards sovereign balance sheets has cushioned private-
sector shocks but narrowed the capacity for future intervention.
Section 4: What are potential candidates for the next crisis?

This section considers some of the current risks in the financial system and whether
they could be sowing the seeds for the next financial crisis. This section is not
designed to be a comprehensive list of financial risks, but rather an assessment of
three important areas where risks have risen in recent years.
1. Could AI spark broader vulnerabilities?
A huge driver of the current equity bull run has been the growth in AI-related stocks.
This report won’t explore the debate as to whether this is a bubble, but examine

whether a correction could eventually propagate into a systemic financial crisis. As
noted earlier, a reversal in the investment cycle need not cause a crisis in itself, and
the bursting of the dot com bubble also did not lead to a broader financial crisis.
Nevertheless, there are issues that could spark vulnerabilities. First, the rapid rise
in AI capex raises questions about whether the investment will pay off. The AI build-
out is on course to exceed the scale of past technological investment cycles, which

have often involved overinvestment even when the technologies ultimately proved
transformative. Second, funding is shifting from predominantly operating cash
flow towards external capital, and rising debt issuance in particular. Third, greater
equity-market concentration increases exposure to a reassessment of technology
valuations. Together, these developments make a correction a clear risk.
To assess the potential for systemic stress, we apply the framework in Greenwood,

Hanson, Shleifer and Sørensen (GHSS). This associates heightened crisis risk with
the combination of a strong run-up in asset prices and excessive credit growth,
estimating a 45% probability of a crisis within 3 years if the two thresholds in Figure
11 are met. On a backward-looking basis as of 2025, an economy-wide assessment
finds that the equity-price increase meets the framework’s “red zone” test, but
business credit growth has been considerably more subdued, given the slowdown

### Page 11

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 11
in credit following the 2022-23 tightening cycle. That is, going into 2026, the pattern
was one of a displacement of credit from other interest-sensitive sectors towards
AI and technology rather than any broad-based rise in leverage.
Nevertheless, US data does show a clear acceleration in credit trends so far in 2026.
To make a more forward-looking assessment, we incorporate the rise in issuance

in 2026 and indicatively extend it into 2027 using the estimated rise in AI capex
plans, assuming credit growth elsewhere in the US economy remains stable
(footnote: We see two-sided risks to this – we may understate the broader credit
demand in tech adjacent areas; conversely, this may understate the drag of higher
rates on credit demand in non-AI related sectors). We also extend the estimated
2027 trend into 2028, including an assumption that equity prices continue their

trend over the past 12 months, to provide an illustrative scenario over the next two
years. Under these assumptions, we would see a visible rise in aggregate US
business sector leverage, leaving the sector just shy of economy-wide red-zone
levels by 2028. As such, approaching that territory by 2028 is conceivable if the AI
cycle continues to accelerate, both in terms of increased debt borrowing and a
renewed rise in valuations.
While the GHSS framework gives a useful quantification of the potential risks, its

indicators also warrant some qualification, which we judge to come in opposing
directions:
n On the one hand, there is a risk that aggregate credit measures may
understate vulnerabilities when borrowing is highly concentrated. Circular
financing, leases and off-balance-sheet structures complicate the picture,
and while we incorporate the recent rise in payables in our leverage metric
to capture some of this risk, it may well be underestimated. Moreover,

realised interest costs remain below current market borrowing rates after
the lengthy period of ultra-low rates, so leverage trends alone may miss the
pressure from rising debt-service costs.
n On the other hand, the price-based metric used by GHSS to assess excess
asset price growth (the 3-year real rise in equity prices) may overstate
valuation excesses given the strong rise in earnings. Indeed, P/E multiples
have already seen a sizeable correction since late 2025, which was most

pronounced for software stocks, and currently there is little evidence of
overly stretched tech valuations, especially when compared to the dot com
boom.
The risk of stress amplification is also likely to depend on the nature of any AI
correction. A downgrading of the AI capex cycle due to slower growth in AI
demand, financing constraints or supply bottlenecks may result in weaker
valuations, leading to negative wealth effects and slower capital expenditure.

However, while this would create an economic headwind, it need not produce a
systemic crisis. And while parallels to the dot com bubble may well be relevant,
currently there are factors that suggest downside risks would be more moderate.
For instance, operating cash flow still plays an important role in financing the AI
buildout, whilst there has been a limited run-up in borrowing and much less
stretched valuations relative to the late-1990s.
Our assessment therefore remains that an AI correction is a clearer risk than an AI-

led systemic crisis at this point. For these risks to become more material, it would
need leverage to keep building but AI returns to eventually disappoint, raising the
likelihood of credit events and amplifying the investment slowdown through the
real economy. We will focus on this interaction between debt-service pressures and

### Page 12

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 12 Deutsche Bank AG
the role of credit events more in the next section.

2. Risks from higher rates and private credit
In the previous section we noted that modest economy-wide debt growth doesn’t
necessarily imply modest credit risk when debt-service costs are rising. As existing
debt reprices or is refinanced, borrowers can face greater pressure even without
additional borrowing. This is a key feature of the debt cycle, whereby vulnerabilities
accumulate during the expansion, and rising rates (often amid a monetary

tightening due to an overheating economy) can then expose them. These risks are
relevant in the context of the current rate hiking cycle, especially as much of the
catch up in debt service costs is yet to play out (see chart below).
With banking-sector risks having moderated since the GFC, the rapid expansion of
private credit and its opaque structure have understandably drawn attention as a
possible source of the next crisis. Parallels with the subprime credit boom have

been periodically made. However, we think these should not be overstated. Even
though the dollar size of the US private credit market is similar to that of subprime
around 2006-07, the market must be assessed relative to the economy (which has
doubled in nominal terms). Moreover, the slower build-up, restrictions on
redemptions and lower risks of direct wealth effects all suggest much more
moderate amplification risks than in 2008.
There are already signs that the private-credit growth cycle is slowing (see chart)

and our credit strategists have noted warning signs including weaker new inflows
into BDCs, increasing payment-in-kind interest and prospective refinancing
pressures. That said, defaults remain limited thus far so these should not be seen
as evidence of an imminent systemic event.

### Page 14

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 14 Deutsche Bank AG
Whether more systemic risks could emerge will depend on issues of solvency,
opacity and capacity for intervention.
n First, while redemption gates can limit immediate cash outflows and
reduce the need to sell illiquid loans under pressure, they do not restore a
borrower’s ability to service debt or absorb underlying credit losses. This
distinction between liquidity protection and solvency is therefore central to

judging whether a slowing credit cycle remains orderly.
n Opacity remains a concern, both in financing structures and in the
distribution of exposures among end-holders, including insurers and other
non-banks. This can make losses harder to identify and potential
amplification harder to assess.
n Policymakers’ success in containing liquidity pressures may not translate
into an effective response to credit events - liquidity provision alone cannot

repair impaired balance sheets. Extending support to opaque non-bank
exposures raises difficult questions about eligibility, loss allocation and the
threshold for intervention.
Quite aside from private credit, the fact that we are back in a rate hiking cycle from
the major central banks also adds to the current risks. Indeed, higher rates bring
down inflation, in part because they tighten financial conditions. But a
consequence of this is that it often reveals vulnerabilities in the financial system,

and makes a crisis more likely. For instance, it was a contributing factor behind the
2023 regional banking crisis, as banks had growing unrealised losses on their
securities portfolio when rates began to rise again. So even if higher rates don’t
directly cause a crisis, they can often be a key contributor. Indeed, hiking cycles
have regularly coincided with some kind of shock, as the experience of recent
decades demonstrates.

3. Stretched fiscal positions and possible NBFI amplifiers
As discussed earlier, the migration of debt towards sovereign balance sheets has

helped cushion private-sector shocks, but this has come at the cost of higher fiscal
risks. Higher public debt and sovereign yields have limited the room for fiscal
stimulus, while persistent inflation has limited the room for monetary stimulus. This
makes another round of coordinated macroeconomic support harder to deliver.
The issue with high public debt is that it if fiscal sustainability is questioned, that can
raise sovereign borrowing costs, which can amplify existing private-sector

vulnerabilities via a tightening in financial conditions and slower growth.
The financial risk posed by sovereign stress will also depend on the financial
amplification channels. These were clear in the European sovereign crisis with the
bank-sovereign loop, but the changing structure of sovereign markets suggests
that the sovereign–NBFI feedback loop may now warrant increasing attention. This
was visible with the 2022 LDI crisis in the UK, while a more recent concern is the run

up in leveraged hedge fund positions in sovereign bond markets, with hedge funds’
sovereign bond exposures more than doubling since 2022 (see chart below). As
noted also by the IMF, this reflects greater issuance and a rising share of sovereign
debt held by hedge funds.
The lessons from Black Monday, LTCM and the LDI crisis offer reassurance that
intervention can contain market stress originating outside the banking system. But
they are not a guarantee that future liquidity shocks will remain economically

benign. The greater scale and interconnectedness of non-bank finance mean that
we can’t simply rely on historical examples today.
Looking forward, a combination of sovereign repricing, leveraged financial
amplification and a more constrained policy response risks proving harder to
resolve than past episodes.

Conclusion
Whilst a major financial crisis has been avoided since the GFC and the subsequent
sovereign crisis, the problem of financial instability has yet to be permanently

solved. On the one hand, reforms to the financial system and the swift actions of
policymakers have helped to prevent a repeat. But as we know from Hyman
Minsky’s warning, lengthy periods of stability can often lay the foundations for the
next crisis, even if these take place slowly. Indeed, risks often shift rather than
disappear.
Looking forward, there are several vectors of risk worth paying attention to. But
financial crises often start in areas where people are paying little attention, or risks

are assumed to be contained. Indeed, it is the areas where risk is underappreciated
which often prove the most vulnerable.
As such, predicting where the next crisis might arise is inherently difficult. But the
topic is a vital one, given the long-lasting damage that financial crises can cause
when they do occur. Indeed, with the fiscal space for sovereigns increasingly
constrained today, the margin for a future policy error is limited.

利差優勢消長，新興與商品
貨幣走勢各異
熱門議題 / 投資關鍵
高油價、美元偏強及全球景氣分化持續牽動新興與
商品貨幣。澳洲就業轉弱、通膨改善，澳洲央行升
息週期料暫告一段落，澳幣短線料區間盤整。紐西
蘭央行意外按兵不動削弱紐幣短期動能，但升息預
期仍提供支撐。加拿大央行政策轉鷹及高油價利好
加幣，惟經濟與貿易不確定性限制升值空間。中國
內需偏弱，但出口暢旺、企業結匯需求強，加上政
策對升值容忍度提高，人民幣料溫和升值。南非幣
受美元、高油價及利差收窄壓抑，但財政紀律改善、
信用前景轉佳及實質利率優勢有助緩和貶值壓力，
料維持區間震盪。

資產 4Q26看法 投資展望

《投資關鍵 – 澳洲景氣放緩、澳央轉向觀望，預期
澳幣盤整》
隨著國際油價維持高位，澳洲央行（RBA）於9月再
度升息將現金利率提高至4.6%，官員雖表示若通膨
壓力持續，不排除進一步緊縮。
但隨著高利率與高通膨壓力持續拖累經濟動能，近期
公布的9月就業意外減少3.93萬人、失業率更升至
4.7%，已連續兩個月落在澳央主席認為可有效壓抑
通膨的失業率4.5~5.0%區間；通膨方面，9月份澳洲
CPI雖由4.0%升至4.3%，但核心CPI僅有2.2%，加以
住房通膨明顯回落，因此本行預期澳央轉向觀望、本
輪升息暫時告終，2027年政策轉向觀望，如國際油
價回落至90美元以下，澳央有降息空間。
外需方面，中國經濟基本面持續低迷，消費與投資不

振亦難以挹注澳洲出口動能，外需對澳幣支撐力量有
限，加以預期聯準會在12月再度升息，將呈現「利
差有撐但優勢遞減」格局，因此預估澳幣短線維持在
0.7~0.71區間盤整，要進一步突破或要待2Q27美國
通膨回落、降息預期再度升溫，方有走揚力量。
重點摘要
• 經濟基本面降溫，核心CPI穩
定，澳洲央行暫停升息轉向
觀望，澳幣維持在0.7~0.71
中性盤整。

紐西蘭通膨升溫訊號浮現，月度物價年增 5.2%
《投資關鍵 - 央行意外按兵不動拖累紐元，後續升息
預期提供下檔支撐》
過去一個月，受美元強勢、美債殖利率及油價走高拖
累，紐幣兌美元一度下探0.5565，其後隨升息預期升
溫而回穩。惟紐西蘭央行10月28日意外維持OCR於
2.75%不變，促使市場下修短期升息預期，紐幣再度
承壓。
紐西蘭經濟延續溫和復甦，企業信心及農產品價格改
善，惟勞動市場仍有閒置產能。雖然整體通膨低於預
期，但核心通膨及價格廣度上升，加上油價高企與紐

幣偏弱，我們預期4Q26通膨仍具黏性，價格壓力擴散
風險猶存。
我們仍維持偏鷹派的政策基準情境。10月以4比3票按
兵不動，較反映升息時點後移，而非整體政策路徑轉
向，緊縮週期尚未結束。我們仍預期12月及明年3月具
升息空間，尤其若通膨、油價及匯率壓力持續，政策
立場料維持偏緊。
展望4Q26，10月意外按兵不動削弱紐幣利差支撐，我
們仍看好紐元中期修復空間，惟短期上行動能有所減
弱。升息預期降溫，加上大選、美元及全球風險情緒
等不確定性，料使匯價維持較高波動。進入1Q27後，
隨政策不確定性逐步下降，經濟溫和復甦有望成為紐
幣主要支撐。考量匯價已反映相當程度的成長疑慮及
外部利空，若基本面持續改善，紐幣中期仍具修復空
間。

### Page 4

通膨數據回落，6月降息的可能性加大重點摘要 《投資關鍵 - 央行轉鷹支撐加幣，惟經濟及貿易風險
限制升值空間》
能源價格高企令加拿大通膨面臨上行風險，加拿大央
行政策預期轉鷹亦推動加美利差劣勢收窄，支持加幣
自10月中旬低點反彈。不過，隨本次25個基點升息
已大致反映於市場定價，加幣能否延續升勢將更取決
於央行對後續緊縮路徑的指引，以及市場對終端利率
的重新定價，而非本次升息本身。
加拿大央行最新升息25個基點至2.5%，為逾三年來
首次升息，並表示若能源價格進一步推升廣泛通膨壓
力，不排除繼續收緊政策。央行亦將2027年通膨預
測由2.0%上調至3.1%，並將今年經濟成長預測由
0.7%上調至1.0%，短期政策訊號偏鷹。惟核心通膨
仍相對溫和，勞動市場、製造業需求及招聘意願亦呈
轉弱跡象，意味若經濟活動進一步降溫，持續升息空
間或有限。高油價及偏高的投機性加幣空頭部位仍構
成短期利好，但美加貿易不確定性可能繼續拖累企業
投資及成長前景。因此，我們維持4Q26至1Q27加幣
中性看法。短期加美利差收窄及油價高企有望限制加

幣下行風險，但在增長前景改善或央行釋出更明確的
持續緊縮訊號前，加幣進一步升值空間有限。

### Page 5

，6月降息的可能性加大重點摘要
• 從景氣趨勢、政策取向與國
際經貿局勢變化三大視角切
入，綜合判斷人民幣溫和升
值的看法不變。
《投資關鍵 – 政經拉鋸與景氣K型下，人民幣溫和升值》
中國公布最新3Q26 GDP成長4.5%，出口年增率升至
28.4%，貿易順差由前季的3148.6億美元擴大至3651.2
億美元。市場預估中國2026 年全年貿易順差將超越
2025年1.2兆美元紀錄，再創新高。惟中國外需繁榮卻
無法掩蓋內需疲軟困境，中國3Q26零售銷售年增率僅
0.7%，固定資產投資衰退7%。儘管中國陸續推出穩增
長政策，大多以財政政策為主軸，貨幣政策偏向定向式
而非全面降準降息。因此儘管美啟動升息，中美利差尚
保持穩定，因此內需疲軟與有限度的寬鬆貨幣政策對人
民幣走勢並未形成顯著壓力。
中國的出口暢旺除受惠科技週期貢獻外，強勁汽車出口
至歐洲也是主因，9月中國汽車出口維持62%的高成長。
惟加劇保護主義傾向，中歐達成初步協議，未來四年內
將對歐洲的混合動力車出口量減少一半以上，具體配額、

最低售價及豁免條件仍待協商。政策角度觀察，人民幣
升值本身可能成為中國應對外部壓力的重要工具，減緩
低估匯率促進出口的國際批評。
考量關稅可能成為攻防戰手段之一，研判中國廠商或搶
出口支持外貿表現高檔，同時官方可能對人民幣升值空
間容忍態度提高，疊加前三季結售匯順差高達3,941億
美元，創2010年以來新高，顯示企業結匯意願強勁，持
續為人民幣提供支撐。從景氣趨勢、政策取向與國際經
貿局勢變化的三大視角切入，綜合判斷人民幣溫和升值
的看法不變。

### Page 6

《投資關鍵 - 基本面改善但部位趨擁擠，南非幣料維
持震盪》
南非幣第四季以來震盪偏弱，USD/ZAR近期回升至約
16.60。外部環境仍偏不利，高油價推升進口成本與
通膨壓力，加上美元偏強及美國南非利差收窄，削弱
南非幣套息吸引力。與此同時，巴西總統大選結果引
發市場波動，增添新興市場不確定性，短期投資人風
險偏好轉趨審慎，亦可能加重新興市場貨幣的資金流
出壓力。
基本面方面，南非財政紀律改善、主權信用前景轉佳，
加上較高實質利率及具吸引力的利差，仍為南非幣提
供支撐。惟高油價及聖嬰現象相關極端天氣風險令通
膨壓力偏高，限制政策寬鬆空間。另一方面，近期投
機性及資產管理機構多頭部位明顯增加，顯示市場部
位漸趨擁擠。在外部風險升溫及風險偏好轉弱下，獲
利了結與部位調整可能放大短期波動，即使匯價回檔，
逢低布局的風險報酬吸引力仍相對有限。
整體而言，南非幣基本面有所改善，但短期外部環境
與部位面均偏不利。財政紀律改善、主權信用前景轉
佳及實質利率優勢有助建立匯率下檔支撐，惟強勢美
元、高油價、利差收窄及偏擁擠的多頭部位限制升值

空間。預期4Q26美元兌南非幣仍以區間震盪為主，維
持南非幣中性看法。
重點摘要
資料來源：CFTC，Bloomberg ，2026/9，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10

## Document 516: インフレ警戒も利上げを急がないＥＣＢ ～長期金利上昇が追加利上げ判断を複雑に～

Source PDF: all report pdf/インフレ警戒も利上げを急がないＥＣＢ ～長期金利上昇が追加利上げ判断を複雑に～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-30
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Europe Trends

2026 年10 月 30 日

1/5 
インフレ警戒も利上げを急がないＥＣＢ 
～長期金利上昇が追加利上げ判断を複雑に～ 
 経済調査部 首席エコノミスト  田中 理 
（℡：050-5474-7494）

（要旨） 
○ ＥＣＢは 10 月 29 日の理事会で、政策金利の据え置きを全会一致で決定した。６月と９月の
利上げ後もエネルギー価格の上昇が続き、物価の上振れリスクが高まっているが、基調的な
インフレが概ね安定し、賃金への二次的波及も限定的なことから、追加利上げを見送った。
前回９月の理事会以降、国債利回りの上昇などで金融環境が引き締まっていることも据え置
き判断に影響した。 
○ 今回の理事会では利上げの是非が議論され、ラガルド総裁は今後も追加利上げを選択肢に残
すことを示唆している。フランスの財政不安による国債利回りの上昇は、金融政策の波及を
妨げるリスクを伴う一方、景気や物価を抑制する効果もある。12 月理事会では、新たな経済
見通しに加え、エネルギー価格の持続性、企業の価格転嫁、賃金動向、金融環境の引き締ま
りが判断材料となる。筆者は 12 月、来年３月、６月の追加利上げで政策金利が 3.25％に達
するとの従来予想を維持するが、利上げの時期や回数を巡る不確実性が高まっている。

■全会一致で据え置きも利上げを議論 
欧州中央銀行（ＥＣＢ）は 10 月 29 日に終わった理事会で、政策金利の据え置きを全会一致で
決定した。ＥＣＢは６月に利上げを再開し、７月に据え置いた後、９月に再び利上げしたが、今
回は追加利上げが見送られた。インフレ懸念が後退したわけではない。９月のユーロ圏の消費者
物価上昇率は前年比＋3.8％と８月の同＋3.2％から加速し、２％の中期的な物価安定を大きく上
回っている。中東情勢の緊迫が長期化するなか、原油や天然ガス価格は９月に発表したＥＣＢの
スタッフ見通しでの想定を大きく上回っており、物価の上振れリスクが高まっている。 
理事会後の記者会見でラガルド総裁は、今回の理事会で利上げが適切かどうかを議論したこと
を明らかにした。それでも今回の利上げを見送ったのは、①物価上昇が今のところエネルギー価
格が中心で、基調的なインフレ率が概ね安定していること、②賃金や他の物価への二次的波及が
限定的なこと、③前回理事会以降の長期金利上昇などで金融環境が引き締まっていること、④エ
ネルギー高が景気を下押しする影響も見極める必要があること―などが背景にある。

### Page 2

2/5 
■金融環境の引き締まりを強調 
 ９月と10 月の声明文を比較すると、物価見通しに対する上振れリスクと景気見通しに対する下
振れリスクを警戒する基本姿勢は変わっていない。ただし、エネルギーショックの規模と持続期
間が拡大し、他の物価への波及が強まる可能性をより明確に指摘している。また、前回理事会以
降に金融環境が引き締まっており、今後の物価と成長を押し下げるとの認識を新たに示した。 
 ９月の声明文では、中東での紛争によってインフレ圧力が継続し、物価上昇率が長期にわたり
目標を上回ると説明していた。これに対して、10 月の声明文では、エネルギー価格がさらに上昇
していることを確認したうえで、エネルギーショックの規模と持続期間が拡大し、間接的・二次
的波及を通じてインフレ率を押し上げ、成長率を押し下げる可能性を指摘した。同時に、基調的
なインフレが概ね安定し、賃金が目立って反応していないことを改めて強調した。物価の上振れ
リスクが高まっているものの、利上げを急ぐ状況には至っていないとの判断が読み取れる。 
 注目されるのが金融環境に関する認識の変化である。９月時点でも市場金利の上昇は確認され
ていたが、10 月の声明文では、前回理事会以降の金融環境の引き締まりが物価と成長を押し下げ
る可能性を政策判断の冒頭で明示している。金利上昇で資金調達コストが上がれば、設備投資や
住宅投資、個人消費が抑制される。ＥＣＢが政策金利を追加的に引き上げなくても、金融環境が
引き締まっていることを意味する。

■財政不安や金融市場の動揺が新たなリスク 
 ＥＣＢはユーロ圏経済について、７～９月期も成長が続いたとの認識を示した。中東紛争の開
始後に落ち込んだサービス業は持ち直し、製造業、デジタルサービス、輸出は人工知能（ＡＩ）
関連の設備投資に支えられている。国防やインフラ関連の政府支出も景気を下支えしている。 
一方、足元のエネルギー価格の再上昇が消費者心理を悪化させており、資金調達コストの上昇
も景気の重荷となりつつある。８月の失業率は低水準を維持したものの、雇用者数や労働力人口
の増加ペースは鈍化している。短期的な景気拡大が続くとしながらも、中東情勢の混乱とエネル
ギー価格の更なる上昇や高止まり、世界的な金融市場の動揺、貿易摩擦の再燃などを背景に、景
気見通しに対するリスクが下振れ方向にあるとの判断を維持した。 
 ９月からの変化として重要なのは、財政の不透明感が金融環境を引き締め、家計や企業の信頼
感を損なうリスクを新たに明確化したことである。声明では特定国を名指ししていないものの、
フランスの財政再建の難航と国債利回りの急上昇が念頭にあるものと考えられる。また、国債市
場の混乱が他国に波及することで金融環境が引き締まり、需要が減少する危険性にも言及した。
エネルギー高が物価を押し上げる一方、長期金利上昇や金融環境の悪化が景気を抑制するとい
う、政策判断の難しい状況が続いている。

■賃金への二次的波及は限定的 
 今回の記者会見で注目されたのが、エネルギー価格の上昇が他の物価や賃金にどの程度波及し
ているかに関する説明である。ＥＣＢは、川上段階ではエネルギー高による物価の押し上げ圧力

### Page 3

3/5 
が表れ始めていると認識している。企業がエネルギー価格の上昇分を製品やサービスの販売価格
に転嫁する間接的な影響は次第に強まっている。ただし、雇用者報酬の伸びや、妥結賃金を集計
したＥＣＢの賃金トラッカーは、今のところ落ち着いている。また、短期の期待インフレ率は上
昇しているものの、長期の期待インフレ率の多くは２％程度で安定している。現時点ではエネル
ギー高が賃金と物価の持続的な上昇を招く悪循環には陥っていない。 
 もっとも、エネルギー価格の高止まりが長期化すれば、企業の価格転嫁が広がり、生活費の上
昇を補う賃上げ要求も強まりやすい。こうした間接的・二次的波及が長期化し、２％目標への物
価の回帰を妨げることを警戒している。

■長期金利上昇で金融環境が引き締まり 
金融環境に関するＥＣＢの認識にも変化がみられる。９月の利上げ後、ユーロ圏の長期金利は
さらに上昇した。背景には、世界的な国債需給の悪化、米国の長期金利上昇、ＥＣＢの保有国債
縮小、各国の財政不安などがある。ラガルド総裁は記者会見で、前回理事会以降に長期の資金調
達コストが明らかに上昇したことを認めた。銀行貸出にも引き締めの影響が表れている。ＥＣＢ
が 10 月27 日に発表した７～９月期の銀行貸出調査によれば、企業向け融資と消費者信用の貸出
基準が一段と厳格化し、融資需要も幅広い分野で減少した。 
追加利上げを判断する際には、こうした金融環境の引き締まりを考慮する必要がある。政策金
利を引き上げなくても、長期金利の上昇や銀行融資の厳格化によって実体経済への引き締め効果
が強まっている。もっとも、成長期待の改善に伴う金利上昇と、財政不安やリスクプレミアムの
拡大による金利上昇では、景気や物価への影響も異なる。ＥＣＢは市場金利の動向だけでなく、
企業や家計の借入金利、銀行の貸出姿勢、金融政策の波及状況を総合的に判断する必要がある。

■フランスの財政不安と利上げ継続の整合性 
 長期金利上昇のなかでも特に注目されるのが、フランスの財政不安である。フランスの10 年国
債利回りは 10 月下旬に５％を超え、ドイツ国債との利回り格差も大幅に拡大した。政府の財政再
建策に対する国民の反発や、議会での予算審議の難航が市場の不安を強めている。今回の声明文
ではフランスを名指ししていないものの、財政の持続可能性が広範な経済安定の重要な支えであ
ると強調した。また、財政の不透明感が金融環境を一段と引き締め、家計や企業の信頼感を損な
う可能性を指摘した。 
 ラガルド総裁は記者会見で、政府債務の大きい国々は、以前よりも高い金利が今後も続く可能
性を財政計画に織り込む必要があると説明し、各国の財政運営がＥＵの財政枠組みに適合するこ
との重要性を強調した。そのうえで、市場の動きが金融政策の適切な波及を脅かす場合、伝達保
護手段（ＴＰＩ）を利用する可能性があることを示唆している。ＴＰＩは各国間の国債利回り格
差の不当で無秩序な拡大に対応するため、2022 年７月に創設された。ＴＰＩは特定国の財政負担
を軽減するための制度ではなく、金融政策の適切な波及を確保するためのものである。財政の持
続可能性などに関する条件もあり、フランス国債の利回りが上昇したからといって自動的に発動

### Page 4

4/5 
されるわけではない。 
フランスの国債市場が一段と混乱した場合、ＥＣＢが量的引き締め（ＱＴ）のペースを緩める
との見方も一部にある。過去の資産買い入れプログラムで保有する国債の償還元本の再投資を再
開し、必要に応じて国別配分を柔軟化することで、市場の安定化を図るとの考え方だ。ただし、
10 月理事会ではＡＰＰ・ＰＥＰＰの再投資を行わず、保有資産の縮小を続ける従来の方針が維持
され、ラガルド総裁も記者会見でＱＴの減速や停止について具体的な方針を示さなかった。ＱＴ
の見直しを正当化するには、保有資産の縮小自体が市場の混乱や金融政策の伝達障害を引き起こ
していることを見極める必要がある。 
フランスの財政不安があるからといって、ＥＣＢが追加利上げを断念するとは限らない。中期
的な物価安定の確保には追加利上げが必要となる一方、特定国で不当で無秩序な市場の混乱が生
じた場合には、金融政策の伝達を守るための手段を別途活用するという政策の組み合わせは可能
である。もっとも、国債売りの背景に財政運営への正当な懸念がある場合、ＴＰＩの発動には慎
重な判断が求められる。フランスの財政再建が進まない場合、利上げを進めながら国債市場の安
定を確保することは、ＥＣＢにとって難しい課題となる。

■12 月に改めて利上げの是非を判断 
ラガルド総裁は理事会後の記者会見で、利上げの是非を実際に議論したことを明らかにした。
また、12 月の次回会合までの間に多くの経済データが発表され、金融政策を判断するうえで重要
な情報が得られると説明した。こうした発言からは、エネルギー価格の上昇が続き、物価の上振
れリスクが強まっている以上、追加利上げが引き続き有力な選択肢となることが示唆される。 
 ただし、先行きの金融政策については、特定の金利経路を予め約束せず、新たに入手可能とな
ったデータに基づき、理事会毎に政策金利を判断する従来からの方針を維持した。また、９月時
点で預金ファシリティ金利は 2.50％に達し、ＥＣＢが様々なモデルで推計する中立金利のレンジ
上限付近にある。追加利上げは、金融政策をより明確な引き締め領域に移すことになるため、中
期的な物価安定の確保に追加の金融引き締めが必要かを十分に検証することが必要となる。 
今回の記者会見で、ラガルド総裁は追加利上げの方向性を示唆する一方、時期やペースを約束
することは避けた。今後の利上げ判断は、エネルギー高による物価上昇圧力と金融環境の引き締
まりによる需要抑制効果を総合的に判断することになる。次回12 月の理事会は、四半期に１回の
スタッフ見通しの発表月で、経済・物価を取り巻く環境をより詳細に検討することになる。

■ラガルド総裁が早期退任 
今回の理事会の結果発表に先立ち、ＥＣＢはラガルド総裁が 2027 年２月７日付で早期退任する
ことを正式に発表した。本来の任期満了である2027 年 10 月末より約９ヶ月早い退任となる。後
任総裁はＥＵ条約で定められた手続きに基づき、欧州理事会によって任命される。早期退任の理
由について、ラガルド総裁は記者会見で、金融政策以外の問題について自由に発言し、活動した
いとの意向を示した。ＥＣＢの幹部には独立性と引き換えに政治的中立性を守る義務があり、発

### Page 5

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
5/5 
言や活動にも制約がある。ただし、退任後の具体的な活動や就任予定の役職を正式に明らかにし
たわけではない。今回の早期退任により、2027 年春のフランス大統領選挙を前に、ＥＵの各国首
脳が後継総裁を選ぶことができる。フランスの政治情勢がＥＣＢ人事に影響することを避けやす
くなるという側面があるが、本人がこれを退任理由として説明したわけではない。 
後任候補としては、オランダ中銀前総裁のクノット氏、スペイン中銀前総裁で国際決済銀行
（ＢＩＳ）の総支配人を務めるデコス氏などの名前が取り沙汰されている。2027 年にはラガルド
総裁に加え、シュナーベル理事、レーン理事も退任する。次期執行部の構成によっては、中長期
的な金融政策の姿勢や市場との対話のあり方に変化が生じる可能性がある。

■利上げ継続を予想も、金融環境の引き締まりに注意 
 12 月16・17 日の次回理事会では、新たなスタッフ見通しが発表される。９月の見通しは８月
19 日までの市場前提に基づいており、その後の原油・天然ガス価格は想定を上回って推移してい
る。12 月のスタッフ見通しでは物価予想がさらに上方修正される可能性が高い。 
ただし、エネルギー高が消費者物価を直接押し上げることだけを理由に、ＥＣＢが利上げを続
けるとは限らない。12 月の判断では、①原油・天然ガス価格の水準と持続性、②企業による価格
転嫁の動向、③賃金への二次的波及、④中長期の期待インフレ率、⑤長期金利上昇と銀行融資の
厳格化、⑥フランスを中心とする国債市場の混乱と他国への波及―などが重要となってこよう。 
筆者は、12 月に25bpの追加利上げを行い、預金ファシリティ金利を 2.75％に引き上げるとの見
通しを維持する。その後も 2027 年３月と６月に25bpずつ追加利上げを実施し、政策金利が
3.25％に達すると予想する。現時点で基調的なインフレや賃金への波及は限定的だが、エネルギ
ー高が長期化するなか、インフレ率が 2028 年まで２％目標を上回る可能性が高いためだ。 
一方、９月時点と比べて、追加利上げを取り巻く環境は複雑になっている。長期金利の上昇が
今後も続き、銀行融資や企業投資、家計消費への悪影響が強まれば、政策金利を引き上げる必要
性は低下する。また、フランスの財政不安が他国の国債市場に飛び火し、銀行システムに波及す
る場合には、ＥＣＢは物価安定と市場の安定を同時に考慮しなければならなくなる。反対に、イ
ラン情勢の緊張が続き、原油・天然ガス価格が一段と上昇すれば、企業の価格転嫁や賃金への波
及が強まり、従来の予想以上に金融引き締めが必要となる可能性もある。 
 インフレ圧力が強まる一方で、金融環境も引き締まるという難しい状況のなか、今回の理事会
では利上げを急がず、新たなデータを見極める姿勢を確認した。今後の金融政策は、エネルギー
価格の上昇による二次的波及と、長期金利上昇を通じた需要抑制効果のどちらが優勢になるかに
左右されることになろう。

以 上

## Document 522: セルビアは政権交代へ

Source PDF: all report pdf/セルビアは政権交代へ.pdf
Report source: MURC
Date: 2026-10-27
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

2026 年 10 月 27 日

MURC Focus

セルビアは政権交代へ

～揺り戻しの中で問われる対中関係のリバランス～

調査部 主任研究員 土田 陽介

1. 総選挙で「学生リスト」が勝利 
西バルカンのセルビアで 10 月 25 日、総選挙（国民議会の一院制、定数 250 議席、比例代表制）が行われた。
即日開票の結果、先に辞任して総選挙に臨んだアレクサンダル・ヴチッチ前大統領は、自身が率いるセルビア進
歩党（SNS）を中心とする与党連合（統一セルビア）の敗北を認めた。代わって、ヴチッチ政権の汚職体質を批判
してきた学生を中心とする政治団体「学生リスト」が、国民の不満の受け皿となって勝利した（図表 1）。

この選挙は、ヴチッチ前大統領に対する、実質的な信任投票の性格を持っていた。経済面では、他国と同様
に、近年のセルビアのインフレは深刻だった。一方、中国や欧州連合（EU）からの相次いだ企業進出や人口動
態に伴う構造的な人手不足、政権による分配政策（最低賃金の引き上げなど）の影響もあって、経済は物価・賃
金スパイラルの様相を呈するに至り、国民の不満は高まっていた（図表 2）。 
図表 1. 開票結果 図表 2. セルビアのインフレと雇用

（注）開票率 96.7%時点。 
（出所）セルビア選管

（出所）セルビア統計局

（年、四半期）
〇西バルカンのセルビアで10月25日、総選挙が行われ、即日開票の結果、ヴチッチ体制への批判を展開して
きた学生を中心とする政治団体「学生リスト」が、国民の不満の受け皿となって勝利した。 
〇新政権に求められることは数多い。外交面では、中国との関係のリバランスが求められるが、セルビアと中
国の経済関係が急速に深化したことも事実であるため、現実的なスタンスが求められる。 
〇経済面では、セルビアが決められない政治に向かうことで、マクロ経済運営の“受動的な健全化”が進むと考
えられる。一方、インフラの維持管理など、能動的な財政政策運営は望みがたい。

### Page 2

政治面では、政権関係者による相次ぐ汚職などが問題視されていた。とりわけヴチッチ政権と中国との近しい
関係が、国民の強い反発を招いた側面が大きい。セルビア憲法では、大統領を務めることができる期間は通算 2
期までとされている。そのため、すでに 2 期を務めたヴチッチ前大統領は辞任のうえ、統一セルビアの首相候補
として総選挙を戦ったが、有権者による信託は得られなかった。

今後セルビアでは、大統領（アナ・ブルナビッチ国民議会議長）が新首相候補を指名し、国民議会の承認を得
たうえで新政権が発足する。現状では、学生リスト所属の議員から新首相が誕生する公算だが、同勢力には明確
な単一リーダーが存在せず、政治経験のないメンバーが数多い。したがって、セルビアは“決められない政治”に
陥る可能性が高い。

2. 問われる対中関係のリバランス 
とはいえ新政権に求められることは数多い。外交面では、中国との関係のリバランスが求められると考えられる。
ヴチッチ政権と中国との近しい関係がセルビア国民の反発を生み、今回の早期総選挙になだれ込んだ以上、新
政権が表立ってヴチッチ政権と同様の親中路線を取ることは難しい。それに、中国と緊張関係にある米国にも、
新政権は配慮しなければならない。

また EU への配慮も欠かせない。ヴチッチ政権は必ずしも EU に敵対的ではなかったが、一方で EU と緊張
関係にある中国のみならず、EU と反目するロシアからも実質的な所得移転（安価なガス供給など）を受けていた
ため、友好的でもなかった。隣国ハンガリーのように親 EU 路線への全面的な転換が果たせれば EU からの所得
移転は着実に増えるだろうが、国内には親中派や親ロ派も少なくないため、配慮が求められる。

図表 3. 対内 FDI ストックの国別内訳の推移 図表 4. 輸出入依存度の推移

### Page 3

それにこの間、セルビアと中国の経済関係が急速に深化したことも事実である。対内 FDI（外国直接投資）の
ストックを確認すると、2020 年から 2025 年の間に、中国からの FDI ストック（図表 3）は約 20 億ユーロから 70 億
ユーロと 3.5 倍も急増しており、ストック全体に占める割合も 4%から 11%まで上昇している。同様に、中国との貿
易依存度も輸入面を中心に上昇が顕著である（図表 4）。

中国企業の多くはハンガリーと国境を接するセルビア北部のヴォイヴォディナ自治州に進出し、対 EU ビジネ
スに備えている。またそうした中国企業との取引を目的に、欧州企業や日本企業も続々と進出している。こうして
形成された産業クラスターが、セルビア全体の経済成長を牽引しているため、新政権もまた中国との友好関係を
維持せざるを得ない。こうした点につき、現実的なスタンスが求められよう。

3. マクロ経済運営は“受動的な健全化”が進む 
経済面では、セルビアが決められない政治に向かうことで、マクロ経済運営の“受動的な健全化”が進むと考え
られる。そもそもセルビアの公的債務残高は、コロナショックを受けた 2021 年をピークに圧縮が進んでおり、2026
年 6 月時点で名目 GDP（国内総生産）の 45%程度にとどまっている（図表 5）。この間に進んだ物価高に伴う“イ
ンフレ税”の存在も大きいが、セルビアの債務は削減が進んでおり、相応に健全な運営がなされている。

制度上、政府による財政拡張を中銀の金融緩和で支えるようなことはできない仕組みとなっていることも、債務
の膨張を阻んでいる。具体的には、セルビア国立銀行（中央銀行、NBS）が物価目標（消費者物価の前年比上
昇率を 3%±1.5%にとどめる）に基づく金融政策運営を維持している。同時に NBS は、通貨ディナールの対ユ
ーロレートを 1 ユーロ＝117～118 ディナールで固定する厳格な通貨政策運営（管理フロート制）を堅持している。

図表 5. セルビアの公的債務残高 図表 6. セルビアの物価と金利

（注）分母の名目 GDP は 4 四半期後方移動累計値。 
（出所）セルビア財務省、同統計局

（注）インフレ目標は 3%±1.5%というレンジ。
